(CI)
gives an equality
\delta_{\lambda}(M^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\varnothing}))=\delta_{\lambda}(\mathbf{T}^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\varnothing}))
.

Now let
\Sigma
be a finite set of primes not containing any primes dividing
N_{\varnothing}Q
, and let
N_{\Sigma}
be the level considered in
[
Dia97
, Section 3.2]
. The inequalities given in the proof of
[
Dia97
, Theorem 3.4]
(which in our case rely on Ihara’s Lemma for the Shimura curves
X^{Q}_{0}(N)
) then show that
\delta_{\lambda}(M^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\Sigma}))\leq\delta_{\lambda}(M^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\varnothing}))
.

But now for each prime
q\in\Sigma
, one has that
R_{q}^{\operatorname{min}}
and
R_{q}^{\square}
are both complete intersections. The work of Section
6
then implies that
\delta_{\lambda}(\mathbf{T}^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\varnothing}))=\delta_{\lambda}(\mathbf{T}^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\Sigma}))
. One then deduces that

\delta_{\lambda}(\mathbf{T}^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\Sigma}))\leq\delta_{\lambda}(M^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\Sigma}))\leq\delta_{\lambda}(M^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\varnothing}))=\delta_{\lambda}(\mathbf{T}^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\varnothing}))=\delta_{\lambda}(\mathbf{T}^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\Sigma}))

and so
\delta_{\lambda}(\mathbf{T}^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\Sigma}))=\delta_{\lambda}(M^{{\mathrm{st}},Q}(N_{\Sigma}))
for all
\Sigma
, generalizing Theorem
(CI)
. By a similar argument, one can also generalize Proposition
(CI)
.

Appendix A
A formula of Venkatesh by N. Fakhruddin and C. Khare

The results of this section are inspired by unpublished notes of
A. Venkatesh
[
Ven16
]
. Venkatesh’s formula was stated (as a
conjecture, but it was checked in many cases) for certain derived
commutative rings, but we prove a version in the context of ordinary
commutative algebra; we briefly explain the connection in Section

A.1
. The invariants
c_{0}
and
c_{1}
are essentially the
same as those defined in
[
Ven16
]
, but our method of proof is
different from the approach taken there. The formula is used in the main text to
compute the Wiles defect for certain Hecke algebras that are not
complete intersections.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be a complete dvr and consider
B
, a complete local
Noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebra with
\dim(B)=1
and with an
augmentation
\pi_{B}:B\to{\mathcal{O}}
. Let
E
be the quotient field of

\mathcal{O}
which we view as a module over any augmented ring using the
augmentation. We assume that the augmentation has a finite cotangent
space, by which we mean that
\ker(\pi_{B})/\ker(\pi_{B})^{2}
is a finite
length
\mathcal{O}
-module . Let
C
be the largest Cohen–Macaulay
quotient of
B
— if
B
is finite over
\mathcal{O}
then this is simply
the quotient of
B
by its
\mathcal{O}
-torsion (which is an ideal)—and
let
\pi_{C}:C\to{\mathcal{O}}
be the augmentation of
C
induced by

\pi_{B}
.

Definition (CI)
.

c_{0}(B):=\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{C}(\Ann(\ker(\pi_{C}))))
.

Since
B
is complete, we may write it as a quotient of

S={\mathcal{O}}[[x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}]]
for some
n\geq 0
. Then by the
prime avoidance lemma (
[
BH93
, Lemma 1.2.2]
), we may find
a quotient
A
of
S
through which the map to
B
factors and such
that
A
is a complete intersection ring with
\dim(A)=1
. Denote
this map
A\to B
by
\phi_{B}
and the induced map
A\to{\mathcal{O}}
by

\pi_{A}
. We may (and do) choose
A
such that

\ker(\pi_{A})/\ker(\pi_{A})^{2}
is a finite length

\mathcal{O}
-module. Furthermore, if
B
is finite over
\mathcal{O}
the lemma
also allows us to choose
A
finite over
\mathcal{O}
.

Let
\mathbf{x}
be a sequence of generators of
\ker(\phi_{B})
of length

\delta
and consider the Koszul complex
8
8

8

We use the notation
and standard properties of the Koszul complex as in

[
BH93
, §1.6]
.

K_{A}(\mathbf{x})
. It is a graded-commutative
differential graded
A
-algebra whose homology modules are

B
-modules. Let
H_{\delta}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))_{1}
be the submodule of

H_{\delta}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))
generated by products of elements of

H_{1}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))
. The Koszul complex is functorial for ring
homomorphisms, so we have a map

\pi_{A,*}:H_{*}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))\to H_{*}(K_{\mathcal{O}}(\overline{\mathbf{x}}))
, where

\overline{\mathbf{x}}
denotes the image of the sequence
\mathbf{x}
in

\mathcal{O}
. However, all terms of this sequence are
0
, so

H_{*}(K_{\mathcal{O}}(\overline{\mathbf{x}}))
is the exterior algebra in
\delta

generators (in homological degree
1
). In particular,

H_{\delta}(K_{\mathcal{O}}(\overline{\mathbf{x}}))\cong{\mathcal{O}}
.

Definition (CI)
.

c_{1}(B):=\ell(\pi_{A,*}(H_{\delta}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x})))/\pi_{A,*}(H_{\delta}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))_{1}))
.

We see that this is finite by localising at the prime ideal
corresponding to the kernel of
\pi_{A}
and observing that this
localisation map factors through
\pi_{A}
.

From the definition of the Koszul complex, it follows that

H_{\delta}(K(\mathbf{x}))
is the annihilator of the ideal
I
generated
by the sequence
\mathbf{x}
. The
A
-submodule of
H_{\delta}(K(\mathbf{x}))

generated by products of elements of
H_{1}(K(\mathbf{x}))
is precisely the
Fitting ideal of
I
(sitting inside its annihilator). It follows that

c_{1}(B)=\ell(\pi_{A}(\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B})))/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(\ker(\phi_{B})))).

(A.1)

We now show that
c_{1}(B)
is independent of all choices. For a fixed

A
as above, the Koszul complex only depends on the number of
generators of the kernel. Moreover, adding more elements in the kernel
to the sequence of generators has the effect of tensoring the Koszul
complex with an exterior algebra in which case it is easy to see that

c_{1}
does not change.

To show that it is independent of the choice of
\phi_{B}:A\to B
, we
will need the following elementary lemma.

Lemma (CI)
.

Let
\mathcal{O}
be any commutative ring,

A_{1},A_{2},B
be local Noetherian
\mathcal{O}
-algebras and

\phi_{i}:A_{i}\to B
,
i=1,2
surjections of
\mathcal{O}
-algebras. Then

1.

A:=A_{1}\times_{B}A_{2}
is also a local Noetherian

\mathcal{O}
-algebra and
\dim(A)=\max\{\dim(A_{1}),\dim(A_{2})\}
.

2.

If
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are complete then so is
A
.

3.

Let
P
be any prime ideal in
B
,
P_{i}=\phi_{i}^{-1}(P)
the
corresponding prime ideals of
A_{i}
and
P_{A}=\phi^{-1}(P)
that
of
A
(where
\phi:A\to B
is the surjection induced by

\phi_{i}
). Then
A_{P_{A}}=(A_{1})_{P_{1}}\times_{B_{P}}(A_{1})_{P_{2}}
.

Proof.

We have

A=\{(a_{1},a_{2})\in A_{1}\times A_{2}:\phi_{1}(a_{1})=\phi_{2}(a_{2})\}
. The ideal
m_{A}
of
A
consisting of all pairs
(a_{1},a_{2})
with

a_{i}\in m_{A_{i}}
is the unique maximal ideal of
A
, since the
surjectivity of
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
implies that the complement
consists of invertible elements, so
A
is local. The two
projections induce surjections
p_{i}:A\to A_{i}
. If
I
is an ideal
of
A
then
p_{1}(I)
is an ideal of
A_{1}
. The kernel of the map

I\to p_{1}(I)
is naturally an ideal of
A_{2}
. Since
A_{1}
and
A_{2}

are Noetherian, this implies that
A
is Noetherian.

Now since
A
is a subring of
A_{1}\times A_{2}
which is finite as an

A
-module (it is generated by
(1,0)
and
(0,1)
), it follows from
the going-up theorem
[
Mat80
, Theorem 5, (i), (ii) and
(iii)]
that

\dim(A)=\dim(A_{1}\times A_{2})=\max\{\dim(A_{1}),\dim(A_{2})\}
.

Suppose
A_{1}
and
A_{2}
are complete. To show that
A
is complete
it suffices to prove that the
m_{A}
-adic topology on
A
is the same
as the topology induced from the inclusion of
A
in

A_{1}\times A_{2}
. Since
m_{A}^{n}\subset m_{A_{1}}^{n}\times m_{A_{2}}^{n}

for all
n>0
, we only need to show that given any
n^{\prime}>0
,

(m_{A_{1}}^{n}\times m_{A_{2}}^{n})\cap A\subset m_{A}^{n^{\prime}}
for all

n\gg 0
. This follows immediately by applying the Artin–Rees
lemma
[
Mat80
, Theorem 15]
, with
I=m_{A}
,

M=A_{1}\times A_{2}
and
N=A
, since

I^{n}M=m_{A_{1}}^{n}\times m_{A_{2}}^{n}
.

We will use the following elementary fact whose simple proof we
skip: If
A
is any commutative ring,
S
any multiplicative subset
of
A
,
M_{1}
,
M_{2}
and
N
any
A
-modules with maps
M_{i}\to N
,

i=1,2
, then the natural map

M_{1}\times_{N}M_{2}\to(M_{2})_{S}\times_{N_{S}}(M_{2})_{S}
of
A
-modules
induces an isomorphism

(M_{1}\times_{N}M_{2})_{S}\to(M_{1})_{S}\times_{N_{S}}(M_{2})_{S}
. The
statement (3) follows from this by taking
M_{i}
to be
A_{i}
,
N
to
be
B
and
S=A\setminus P_{A}
and by observing that

A_{i}\otimes_{A}A_{P_{A}}=(A_{i})_{P_{i}}
,
i=1,2
and

B\otimes_{A}A_{P_{A}}=B_{P}
.

∎

It follows from Lemma
(CI)
and the prime avoidance lemma
already used earlier, that if
A_{i}
are complete intersections of the
same dimension with surjections to
B
, then both of them may be
dominated by a complete intersection
A^{\prime}
of the same dimension. The
condition on the finiteness of the cotangent space can also be
preserved by (3) of Lemma
(CI)
. For the independence of the choice
of
A
in the definition of
c_{1}(B)
we will also need:

Lemma (CI)
.

Let
f:A^{\prime}\to A
be a surjection of (complete) complete intersection
local rings and let
\phi_{B}:A\to B
be any surjection of rings. Let

\mathbf{z}
be any finite sequence of generators of
\ker(f)
,
\mathbf{x}

any sequence of generators of
\ker(\phi_{B})
, and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
a lift
of
\mathbf{x}
to
A^{\prime}
. Then
H_{*}(K_{A^{\prime}}((\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))
is
isomorphic to
H_{*}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))
tensored with an exterior algebra
over
A
with
|\mathbf{z}|+\dim(A)-\dim(A^{\prime})
free generators.

Proof.

Let
g:S\to A^{\prime}
be a surjection from a regular local ring
S

(which exists because
A^{\prime}
is complete), so both
\ker(g)
and

\ker(fg)
are generated by regular sequences. Choose a sequence of
generators
{\mathbf{y}}
of
\ker(\phi_{B}fg)
by first choosing a
regular sequence of generators
\mathbf{w}
of
\ker(g)
and then adding
lifts
\tilde{\mathbf{z}}
of elements of
\mathbf{z}
and lifts

\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}
of lifts
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
in
A^{\prime}
of elements of

\mathbf{x}
. We then set

\mathbf{y}=(\mathbf{w},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},\tilde{\mathbf{z}})
and consider

K_{S}(\mathbf{y})
. Since the Koszul complex of a regular sequence is a
resolution of the corresponding quotient ring, by applying this to

\mathbf{w}
we see that
K_{S}(\mathbf{y})
is quasi-isomorphic (as a
differential graded
S
-algebra) to

K_{S}((\tilde{\mathbf{z}},\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}))\otimes_{S}A^{\prime}
, i.e.,

K_{A^{\prime}}((\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))
. On the other hand, since
A
is also
complete intersection ring, by choosing a minimal generating set of

\ker(fg)
from among the elements of
(\mathbf{w},\tilde{\mathbf{z}})
,
one sees that
K_{S}(\mathbf{y})
is quasi-isomorphic to
K_{A}(\mathbf{x})

tensored with an exterior algebra (since

gf(\tilde{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})=\mathbf{x}
and the remaining elements of

(\mathbf{w},\tilde{\mathbf{z}})
become
0
in
A
). On taking homology we
see that
H_{*}(K_{A^{\prime}}((\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))
is isomorphic to

H_{*}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))
tensored with an exterior algebra. The number of
free generators of this exterior algebra is easily seen to be

|\mathbf{z}|+\dim(A)-\dim(A^{\prime})
since

|\mathbf{w}|=\dim(S)-\dim(A^{\prime})
.

∎

Lemma (CI)
.

The invariant
c_{1}(B)
is well-defined.

Proof.

By Lemma
(CI)
and the remarks following it, it suffices to
show that if
\phi_{B}:A\to B
is as above and we have a surjection

f:A^{\prime}\to A
such that
\phi_{B}^{\prime}:=\phi_{B}f
also satisfies the
conditions analogous to those imposed on
\phi_{B}
, then the numbers
defined using
\phi_{B}
and
\phi_{B}^{\prime}
are equal.

Let
\mathbf{x}
be a sequence of generators of
\ker(\phi_{B})
,

\mathbf{x}^{\prime}
a lift of this sequence to
A^{\prime}
and
\mathbf{z}
a sequence of
generators of
\ker(f)
. Let
\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}^{\prime})
, so

\mathbf{w}
is a sequence of generators of
\ker(\phi_{B}^{\prime})
. Thus,

\delta=|\mathbf{x}|
and
\delta^{\prime}
, the corresponding number of
generators for
\ker(\phi_{B}^{\prime})
, equals

|\mathbf{w}|=\delta+|\mathbf{z}|
. Note that

H_{\delta^{\prime}}(K_{A^{\prime}}((\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))
is canonically isomorphic to

\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B}^{\prime}))
and
H_{\delta}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))
is canonically
isomorphic to
\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B}))
. From the result of Lemma

(CI)
(specialised to the case
\dim(A)=\dim(A^{\prime})
)
that
H_{*}(K_{A^{\prime}}((\mathbf{z},\mathbf{x}^{\prime}))
is isomorphic to

H_{*}(K_{A}(\mathbf{x}))
tensored with an exterior algebra over
A
with

|\mathbf{z}|
free generators, it follows that there is an isomorphism
of
A
-modules
\alpha:\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B}^{\prime}))\to\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B}))

such that
\alpha(\Fitt(\ker(\phi_{B}^{\prime})))=\Fitt(\ker(\phi_{B}))
.

Now we use the finite cotangent space assumption on
A
and

A^{\prime}
. This implies that
\pi_{A}(\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B})))\subset\mathcal{O}

is nonzero and equal to the image of

\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B}))\otimes_{A}\mathcal{O}
in

A\otimes_{A}\mathcal{O}=\mathcal{O}
(and similarly for
A^{\prime}
and also for
the Fitting ideals). The
\mathcal{O}
-module

\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B}))\otimes_{A}\mathcal{O}
modulo its torsion is free of
rank one (and similarly for
A^{\prime}
) so the lemma follows from
(
A.1
) and the above by using the isomorphism

\alpha\otimes_{A}\mathcal{O}
.

∎

For any map of rings
R_{1}\to R_{2}
, an
R_{2}
-module
M
and

i\geq 0
, we denote by
\Der^{i}_{R_{1}}(R_{2},M)
the
i
-th
André–Quillen cohomology group of
R_{2}
with coefficients in
M
.
Let
E
denote the quotient field of
\mathcal{O}
viewed as a
B
-module
via
\pi_{B}
.

The invariants
c_{0}(B)
and
c_{1}(B)
defined above are linked by the
following proposition, which may be viewed as a derived version of
Wiles’s formula for complete intersections
[
Wil95
]
,

[
Len95
]
,
[
FKR21
, §A]
; a variant of this formula was first
discovered by A. Venkatesh
[
Ven16
]
.

Proposition (CI)
.

c_{0}(B)-c_{1}(B)=\ell(\Der^{0}_{\mathcal{O}}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}}))-\ell(\Der^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}})).

(A.2)

Proof.

We denote by
J
the ideal
\ker(\phi_{B})
with
\phi_{B}:A\to B
as
above. The sequence of maps
{\mathcal{O}}\to A\to B
gives rise to an
exact sequence of André–Quillen cohomology

0\to\Der^{0}_{\mathcal{O}}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}})\to\Der^{0}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,E/{\mathcal{O}})\to\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(J/J^{2},E/{\mathcal{O}})\to\Der^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}})\to 0.

(A.3)

The
0
on the left comes from the fact that
\Der^{0}_{A}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}})=0

since
\phi_{B}
is surjective (which also gives that

\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(J/J^{2},E/{\mathcal{O}})
is equal to
\Der^{1}_{A}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}})
). The
0
on
the right comes from the fact that

\Der^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,E/{\mathcal{O}})=\Der^{2}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,{\mathcal{O}})=0
, where the first equality
is because
\Der^{i}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,E)=0
for all
i
(a consequence of the
finite tangent space condition on
\pi_{A}
) and the second follows from

[
Avr99
, (1.2) Theorem]
because
A
is a complete intersection,

\mathcal{O}
is regular, and we have a surjection from
S
onto
A
.

We claim that
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(J/J^{2},E/{\mathcal{O}})
and
\Der^{0}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,E/{\mathcal{O}})
are finite length
{\mathcal{O}}
-modules and that we have equalities
\ell(\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(J/J^{2},E/{\mathcal{O}}))=\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(J)))
and
\ell(\Der^{0}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,E/{\mathcal{O}}))=\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(\ker(\pi_{A}))))
. Assuming the claim, from sequence (
A.3
) we deduce

\ell(\Der^{0}_{\mathcal{O}}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}}))-\ell(\Der^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}}))=\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(\ker(\pi_{A}))))-\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(J))).

(A.4)

By definition
c_{0}(B)=\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{C}(\Ann(\ker(\pi_{C}))))
,
c_{1}(B)=\ell(\pi_{A}(\Ann(\ker(\phi_{B})))/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(\ker(\phi_{B}))))
by (
A.1
), and
Lemma
(CI)
below implies that

\ell(\mathcal{O}/\pi_{A}(\Ann_{A}(\ker(\pi_{A}))))=\ell(\mathcal{O}/\pi_{A}(\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B}))))+\ell(\mathcal{O}/\pi_{C}(\Ann_{C}(\ker(\pi_{C})))).

Recalling that
J=\ker(\phi_{B})
and

\Fitt(\ker(\pi_{A}))=\Ann_{A}(\ker(\pi_{A}))
(since
A
is a complete
intersection), the proposition follows by inserting these three
equalities in (
A.4
).

We now prove the claim made above: For the first part, note that
E/{\mathcal{O}}
is an
A
-module via
\pi_{A}
, so that
\mathrm{Hom}_{A}(J/J^{2},E/{\mathcal{O}})\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{O}}(J/J^{2}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}},E/{\mathcal{O}})
, where
{\mathcal{O}}
is an
A
-module via
\pi_{A}
, and it suffices to show that
J/J^{2}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
is a finite length
{\mathcal{O}}
-module. The module
J/J^{2}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
is of finite type over
{\mathcal{O}}
because
J/J^{2}
is of finite type over
A
, and so we need to show that
J/J^{2}[1/\varpi]\otimes_{A[1/\varpi]}E
vanishes. Now the map
\phi[1/\varpi]:A[1/\varpi]\to B[1/\varpi]
is a map of finite-dimensional
E
-algebras and the (compatible) augmentations to
\pi_{A}[1/\varpi]
and
\pi_{B}[1/\varpi]
give rise to isomorphisms of a single factor with
E
, i.e.,
\pi_{A}[1/\varpi]\otimes_{A[1/\varpi]}E
is an isomorphism and
J[1/\varpi]\otimes_{A[1/\varpi]}E=0
because
J[1/\varpi]
must then be supported on the other factors, and hence
J/J^{2}[1/\varpi]\otimes_{A[1/\varpi]}E=0
. For the second part we apply the conormal sequence to
{\mathcal{O}}\to A\to{\mathcal{O}}
which gives the isomorphism
\ker(\pi_{A})/\ker(\pi_{A})^{2}\cong\Omega_{A/{\mathcal{O}}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
due to the splitting of
A\to{\mathcal{O}}
. By construction the right hand term in the isomorphism is of finite
{\mathcal{O}}
-length, and the second part now follows from
\Der^{0}_{\mathcal{O}}(A,E/{\mathcal{O}})\cong\Hom_{A}(\Omega_{A/{\mathcal{O}}},E/{\mathcal{O}})\cong\Hom_{\mathcal{O}}(\Omega_{A/{\mathcal{O}}}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}},E/{\mathcal{O}})
.

For the first assertion on lengths we need to show that
J/J^{2}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(J))
have the same lengths. Because
\pi_{A}(J)=0
, the image of
J^{2}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
in
J\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
is zero, and hence
J/J^{2}\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}\cong J\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
. Next observe that
\pi_{A}(\Fitt(J))=\Fitt(J\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}})
, as follows from the definition of the Fitting ideal. The equality of length now follows because for a finite length
{\mathcal{O}}
-module over the dvr
{\mathcal{O}}
the theory of elementary divisors gives
\ell(M)=\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\Fitt(M))
. The argument for the second length equality proceeds in the same way. One reduces the equality to showing that
\ker(\pi_{A})/\ker(\pi_{A})^{2}\cong\ker(\pi_{A})\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}}
and
{\mathcal{O}}/\Fitt(\ker(\pi_{A})\otimes_{A}{\mathcal{O}})
have the same length.
∎

Remark (CI)
.

The above proof shows in particular, that the terms
\Der^{1}_{\mathcal{O}}(B,E/{\mathcal{O}})
and
{\mathcal{O}}/\pi_{A}(\Fitt(J))
are of finite
{\mathcal{O}}
-length.

Remark (CI)
.

If
B
is a complete intersection in Proposition
(CI)
we may
take
A=B
, so
c_{1}(B)=0
,
c_{0}(B)=\ell({\mathcal{O}}/\eta_{B})
and
(
A.4
) shows that Proposition
(CI)
reduces to Wiles’s
formula. The proposition shows once again that
c_{1}(B)
is
independent of all choices since all the other terms in the formula
are clearly so.

The following lemma was used in the proof of Proposition
(CI)
.

Lemma (CI)
.

Let
A
be a Gorenstein local ring with an augmentation

\pi_{A}:A\to\mathcal{O}
such that
\ell(\Phi_{A})<\infty
. Assume that

\pi_{A}
factors through a surjective ring homomorphism

\phi_{B}:A\to B
and let
C
be the largest quotient of
B
which
is Cohen–Macaulay, so there are surjections
\phi_{C}:A\to C
,

\pi_{B}:B\to\mathcal{O}
and
\pi_{C}:C\to\mathcal{O}
. Then

\pi_{A}(\Ann_{A}(\ker(\pi_{A})))=\pi_{A}(\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B})))\,\pi_{C}(\Ann_{C}(\ker(\pi_{C}))).

Proof.

We may apply Lemma A.10 of
[
FKR21
]
to the map
\phi_{C}
, since
C
is
Cohen–Macaulay, to deduce that

\pi_{A}(\Ann_{A}(\ker(\pi_{A})))=\pi_{A}(\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{C})))\,\pi_{C}(\Ann_{C}(\ker(\pi_{C}))),

so it suffices to to prove that

\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{C}))=\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B}))
. We have

\ker(\phi_{B})\subset\ker(\phi_{C})
and the quotient is a finite
length
A
-module by the definition of
C
. The quotient map

\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B}))\ker(\phi_{C})\to\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B}))(\ker(\phi_{C})/\ker(\phi_{B}))

is an isomporphism since
\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B}))\ker(\phi_{B})=(0)
, so

\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B}))\ker(\phi_{C})
, being a submodule of a finite
length
A
-module, is also of finite length. On the other hand, it is
a submodule of
A
and
{\rm depth}(A)=1
, so it
must be
(0)
. Thus,
\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{C}))=\Ann_{A}(\ker(\phi_{B}))
.
∎

A.1

We briefly explain how the formula (
A.2
) can be viewed as a derived version of Wiles’s formula:

Suppose we have a presentation

B=\mathcal{O}[[x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}]]/(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n+\delta})
with

\delta\geq 0
. We may use this to construct a “derived” ring

\mathcal{B}=\mathcal{O}[[x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}]]\ {\otimes}_{\mathcal{O}[[y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n},\dots,y_{n+\delta}]]}\ \mathcal{O},

where the tensor product is defined as in
[
GV18
, Definition
3.3]
. Here the
x_{i},y_{j}
are in “degree 0” and the map
from
\mathcal{O}[[y_{1},y_{2},\dots,y_{n},\dots,y_{n+\delta}]]
to

\mathcal{O}[[x_{1},x_{2},\dots,x_{n}]]
is given by
y_{j}\mapsto f_{j}
and to

\mathcal{O}
by
y_{j}\mapsto 0
.

If we assume that
A=\mathcal{O}[[x_{1},\dots,x_{n}]]/(f_{1},f_{2},\dots,f_{n})

is a one dimensional complete intersection, then the derived ring has
“defect” equal to
\delta
.
The invariant
c_{1}(B)
may then be viewed as coming from

\pi_{*}(\mathcal{B})
, since this may be computed in terms of a Koszul
complex. Venkatesh views (
A.2
) as an analogue of
Wiles’s formula for the derived ring
\mathcal{B}
, which is a “derived
complete intersection”. (However, as we have shown, all the terms in
the formula only depend on
B=\pi_{0}(\mathcal{B})
, so it may also be
viewed as a generalisation of Wiles’s formula to rings which are not
necessarily complete intersections.)
